Numerical scheme derivation

In this section, we discuss numerical scheme for Fractional Epidemiological Model using Newton Interpolation Method.

Fractional system formulation

Consider a general Cauchy problem with the Caputo-Fabrizio fractional differential operator: 24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{cases}{}^{\mathfrak{CF}}\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{t}}\Upsilon(\mathsf{t})=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t},\Upsilon(\mathsf{t}))\\\Upsilon(0)=\Upsilon_{0},\end{cases}$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e} \in (0,1)$$\end{document} is the fractional order, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e}) > 0$$\end{document} is a normalization function with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{G}(0)=\mathcal{G}(1)=1$$\end{document}.

Applying the associated fractional integral operator yields: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\Upsilon(\mathsf{t})&=\Upsilon(0)+\frac{2(1-\mathsf{e})}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t},\Upsilon(\mathsf{t}))+\frac{2\mathsf{e}}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}\int_{0}^{\mathsf{t}}\mathcal{Z}(\xi,\Upsilon(\xi))d\xi.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Discretization

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}} = \mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{h}$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{h} > 0$$\end{document} is the step size, and denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}} \approx \Upsilon(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}})$$\end{document}. Evaluating at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}$$\end{document}: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}+1}&=\Upsilon(0)+\frac{2(1-\mathsf{e})}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}},\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}})+\frac{2\mathsf{e}}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}\int_{0}^{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}}\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t},\Upsilon(\mathsf{t}))d\mathsf{t}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

The difference between successive terms gives: 25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}+1}-\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}}&=\frac{2(1-\mathsf{e})}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}},\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}})-\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}-1}))\nonumber\\&\quad+\frac{2\mathsf{e}}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}\int_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}}^{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}}\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t},\Upsilon(\mathsf{t}))d\mathsf{t}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Newton interpolation approximation

To approximate the integral in (25), we employ Newton’s forward difference formula. On the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}, \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}]$$\end{document}, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t},\Upsilon(\mathsf{t}))$$\end{document} is approximated by a quadratic Newton polynomial using three points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-2}, \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-2}), (\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}, \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}), (\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}, \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}})\}$$\end{document}:

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{m}} = \mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{m}}, \Upsilon_{\mathfrak{m}})$$\end{document}. The Newton forward polynomial of degree 2 is: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}P_2(\mathsf{t}) &= \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-2} + \frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1} - \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-2}}{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathsf{t} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-2}) \\&\quad + \frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} - 2\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1} + \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-2}}{2\mathfrak{h}^2}(\mathsf{t} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-2})(\mathsf{t} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}).\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

However, for an explicit two-step method, we use linear interpolation over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}, \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}]$$\end{document} and extrapolate to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}, \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}]$$\end{document}. The linear Newton polynomial through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}, \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1})$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}, \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}})$$\end{document} is: 26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_1(\mathsf{t}) = \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1} + \frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} - \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}}{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathsf{t} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}).$$\end{document}

Using (26) to approximate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t},\Upsilon(\mathsf{t}))$$\end{document} over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}, \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}]$$\end{document}, we compute: 27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\int_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}}^{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}} \mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t},\Upsilon(\mathsf{t}))d\mathsf{t} &\approx \int_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}}^{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}} P_1(\mathsf{t}) d\mathsf{t} \nonumber \\&= \int_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}}^{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}} \left[ \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1} + \frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} - \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}}{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathsf{t} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}) \right] d\mathsf{t} \nonumber \\&= \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}\mathfrak{h} + \frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} - \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}}{\mathfrak{h}} \int_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}}^{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}} (\mathsf{t} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}) d\mathsf{t}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Evaluating the integral: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\int_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}}^{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}} (\mathsf{t} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}) d\mathsf{t} &= \left[ \frac{1}{2}(\mathsf{t} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1})^2 \right]_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}}^{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}} \\&= \frac{1}{2}\left[(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1})^2 - (\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}} - \mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1})^2\right] \\&= \frac{1}{2}\left[(2\mathfrak{h})^2 - (\mathfrak{h})^2\right] = \frac{1}{2}(4\mathfrak{h}^2 - \mathfrak{h}^2) = \frac{3\mathfrak{h}^2}{2}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Substituting back into (27): 28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\int_{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}}}^{\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}+1}} \mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{t},\Upsilon(\mathsf{t}))d\mathsf{t} &\approx \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}\mathfrak{h} + \frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} - \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}}{\mathfrak{h}} \cdot \frac{3\mathfrak{h}^2}{2} \nonumber \\&= \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}\mathfrak{h} + \frac{3\mathfrak{h}}{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} - \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}) \nonumber \\&= \frac{3\mathfrak{h}}{2}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} - \frac{\mathfrak{h}}{2}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Numerical scheme

Substituting (28) into (25) gives: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}+1} &= \Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}} + \frac{2(1-\mathsf{e})}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} - \mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}) \\&\quad + \frac{2\mathsf{e}}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}\left(\frac{3\mathfrak{h}}{2}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} - \frac{\mathfrak{h}}{2}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}\right).\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Simplifying: 29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}+1} = \Upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}} + A\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}} + B\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}A &= \frac{2(1-\mathsf{e})}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})} + \frac{3\mathsf{e}\mathfrak{h}}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}, \\B &= -\frac{2(1-\mathsf{e})}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})} - \frac{\mathsf{e}\mathfrak{h}}{(2-\mathsf{e})\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{e})}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Application to epidemiological HCV model

Consider the six-compartment model: 30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{cases}\mathcal{S}\prime &= \mathfrak{b} - (\mu + \lambda)\mathcal{S} + \phi\mathcal{R}, \\\mathcal{E}\prime &= \lambda\mathcal{S} - (\mu + \gamma)\mathcal{E}, \\\mathcal{A}\prime &= \gamma\mathcal{E} - (\mu + \mathfrak{r})\mathcal{A}, \\\mathcal{C}\prime &= (1-\eta)\mathfrak{r}\mathcal{A} - \delta(1-\theta)\mathcal{C} - (\rho + \mu + \mathsf{v}_{1})\mathcal{C}, \\\mathcal{Q}_t\prime &= \delta(1-\theta)\mathcal{C} - (\alpha\rho + \mu + \mathsf{v}_{2})\mathcal{Q}_t, \\\mathcal{R}\prime &= (\mathcal{C} + \alpha\mathcal{Q}_t)\rho + \eta\mathfrak{r}\mathcal{A} - (\mu + \phi)\mathcal{R},\end{cases}$$\end{document}

Component-wise numerical scheme

Applying the scheme (29) to each compartment: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}+1} &= \mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}} + A\mathcal{Z}_1(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}) \\&\quad + B\mathcal{Z}_1(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}), \\\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}+1} &= \mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}} + A\mathcal{Z}_2(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}) \\&\quad + B\mathcal{Z}_2(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}), \\\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}+1} &= \mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}} + A\mathcal{Z}_3(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}) \\&\quad + B\mathcal{Z}_3(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}), \\\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}+1} &= \mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}} + A\mathcal{Z}_4(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}) \\&\quad + B\mathcal{Z}_4(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}), \\\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}+1} &= \mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}} + A\mathcal{Z}_5(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}) \\&\quad + B\mathcal{Z}_5(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}), \\\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}+1} &= \mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}} + A\mathcal{Z}_6(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}) \\&\quad + B\mathcal{Z}_6(\mathsf{t}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{Q}_{t\mathfrak{n}-1},\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{n}-1}),\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

where the kernel functions are: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathcal{Z}_1 &= \mathfrak{b} - (\mu + \lambda)\mathcal{S} + \phi\mathcal{R}, \\\mathcal{Z}_2 &= \lambda\mathcal{S} - (\mu + \gamma)\mathcal{E}, \\\mathcal{Z}_3 &= \gamma\mathcal{E} - (\mu + \mathfrak{r})\mathcal{A}, \\\mathcal{Z}_4 &= (1-\eta)\mathfrak{r}\mathcal{A} - \delta(1-\theta)\mathcal{C} - (\rho + \mu + \mathsf{v}_1)\mathcal{C}, \\\mathcal{Z}_5 &= \delta(1-\theta)\mathcal{C} - (\alpha\rho + \mu + \mathsf{v}_2)\mathcal{Q}_t, \\\mathcal{Z}_6 &= (\mathcal{C} + \alpha\mathcal{Q}_t)\rho + \eta\mathfrak{r}\mathcal{A} - (\mu + \phi)\mathcal{R},\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Graphical visualization

During this section, we will be performing numerical simulations on the parameters of our model showed in Table 2. For the purpose of applying the referenced parameters (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{b}$$\end{document}, µ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{v}_{1}$$\end{document}, β2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi $$\end{document}, η, and δ), their respective ranges from the literature were utilized [98]. For the purpose of ensuring that the model closely matched HCV data from Zimbabwe (1990–2019) with an RMS error of 0.0001, the slider feature of Berkeley Madonna was utilized to locate the parameters that were fitted. which can be obtained from: https://www.globalhep.org/country-progress/zimbabwe [110], using initial conditions as follows \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} Model parameters and their interpretations

The dynamics of the susceptible population, showed in Fig. 7, provide critical insight into the decrease rate of healthy individuals. The susceptible population declines most rapidly, reaching its lowest equilibrium swiftly. In stark contrast, as the fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} decreases, the descent of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{S}$$\end{document} curve becomes significantly more gradual and converge after 30 years. This indicates a system crucial factor for long-term epidemic forecasting and the planning of treatment or public awareness campaigns.Fig. 7Susceptible (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{S}$$\end{document}) population dynamics for different fractional order

Susceptible (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{S}$$\end{document}) population dynamics for different fractional order

The behavior of the exposed compartment (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{E}$$\end{document}), shown in Fig. 8, (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} = 1.0) predicts a sharp, high-magnitude peak upto 18 years for lower fractional order and the curve decrease of slowly for higher fractional order, which suggesting a rapid and intense initial outbreak. However, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} decreases, the system’s memory dampens this response and converge after 50 years. Therefore, accurately estimating the appropriate fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} for a specific population is essential for designing effective intervention strategies, whether they aim to mitigate a sharp peak or manage a persistent, endemic state.Fig. 8Exposed (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{E}$$\end{document}) population dynamics for different fractional order

Exposed (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{E}$$\end{document}) population dynamics for different fractional order

The trajectories of the acute (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{A}$$\end{document}) in Fig. 9 and chronic (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{C}$$\end{document}) populations in Fig. 10 shows the critical impact of fractional order on disease progression. For lower values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document}, the peak of the acute compartment is peak upto 25 years and decline slowly and converge after 50 years, indicating a slower progression from exposure to symptomatic acute infection. Consequently, the subsequent influx into the chronic compartment (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{C}$$\end{document}) is also slower and more gradual and similarly converge after 50 years. The integer-order model (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} = 1.0), by contrast, predicts a sharper, more severe acute outbreak that rapidly seeds a larger chronic population. This has direct implications for healthcare planning, as a lower \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} would predict a less sudden but more sustained demand for diagnostic and management resources for chronic HCV.Fig. 9Acute (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{A}$$\end{document}) population dynamics for different fractional orderFig. 10Chronic (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{C}$$\end{document}) population dynamics for different fractional order

Acute (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{A}$$\end{document}) population dynamics for different fractional order

Chronic (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{C}$$\end{document}) population dynamics for different fractional order

The fractional order critically influences the ultimate success of treatment interventions, as evidenced by the quit treatment (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{Q}_{t}$$\end{document}) in Fig. 11 and recovered (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{R}$$\end{document}) compartments in Fig. 12. A lower order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} results in a slower accumulation of individuals who quit treatment (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{Q}_t$$\end{document}) and curve is goes at peak om 30 years, then decline and converge after 50 years, implying a system where the decision to discontinue therapy is influenced by longer-term historical factors, leading to a more protracted but less voluminous trend of treatment failure. Most significantly, the recovery process is drastically altered. The classical model (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} = 1.0) shows a rapid rise in recoveries, reaching a high equilibrium quickly and converge at 40 years. As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} decreases, the path to recovery becomes a much slower, more gradual climb and converge after 50 years for lower values of fractional order, reflecting the cumulative and memory-dependent nature of successful long-term treatment outcomes and immunity. The final recovered population equilibrium is highly sensitive to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document}, indicating that the long-term effectiveness of control programs is intrinsically tied to these system memory properties.Fig. 11Quit treatment (Qt) population dynamics for different fractional orderFig. 12Recovery (R) population dynamics for different fractional order

Quit treatment (Qt) population dynamics for different fractional order

Recovery (R) population dynamics for different fractional order

The unified response across all model compartments solidifies the role of the fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} as a master regulator of epidemic tempo and outcome in Fig. 13. Lower values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}$$\end{document} depict an epidemic characterized by slower transmission, delayed peaks, dampened outbreak intensity, and a prolonged but steady journey toward recovery and control. The Fig. 14 shows the three dimensional visualization of different fractional order effects for HCV dynamics. Our findings strongly advocate for the adoption of fractional calculus frameworks to develop more robust and realistic public health strategies for managing HCV and similar complex infectious diseases.Fig. 13HCV model dynamic of all compartment

Physics-informed neural networks: bridging machine learning and mechanistic modeling

Conceptual foundation and theoretical motivation

The paradigm shift in computational epidemiology

Traditional approaches to epidemiological modeling face fundamental limitations:Numerical Methods: Finite difference/element methods require dense discretization and struggle with inverse problemsData-Driven ML: Pure neural networks lack physical interpretability and require massive datasetsParameter Estimation: Conventional methods often decouple state estimation from parameter identification

Numerical Methods: Finite difference/element methods require dense discretization and struggle with inverse problems

Numerical Methods: Finite difference/element methods require dense discretization and struggle with inverse problems

Data-Driven ML: Pure neural networks lack physical interpretability and require massive datasets

Data-Driven ML: Pure neural networks lack physical interpretability and require massive datasets

Parameter Estimation: Conventional methods often decouple state estimation from parameter identification

Parameter Estimation: Conventional methods often decouple state estimation from parameter identification

Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) represent a transformative approach that seamlessly integrates mechanistic knowledge with data-driven learning. For HCV modeling, this enables: 31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm{PINN} = \underbrace{\text{Neural Network}}_{\text{Universal Approximator}} + \underbrace{\text{HCV Transmission Dynamics}}_{\text{Physical Constraints}}$$\end{document}

Mathematical formulation of the HCV transmission problem

The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{S}(t)\mathcal{E}(t)\mathcal{A}(t)\mathcal{C}(t)\mathcal{Q}_{t}(t)\mathcal{R}(t)$$\end{document} model defines a dynamical system: 32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt} = \mathbf{f}(t, \mathbf{u}; \boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{epi}}), \quad t \in [0, T]$$\end{document}33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf{u}(0) = \mathbf{u}_0$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf{u}(t) = [\mathcal{S}(t), \mathcal{E}(t), \mathcal{A}(t), \mathcal{C}(t), \mathcal{Q}_{t}(t), \mathcal{R}(t)]^T \in \mathbb{R}^6$$\end{document} represents population compartments and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{epi}}$$\end{document} denotes epidemiological parameters.

PINN methodology

The flowchart in Fig. 15 illustrates the iterative training process of Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) for epidemiological modeling. The methodology begins with data collection and network architecture design which contains hidden layer, followed by the computation of both physics loss (incorporating disease dynamics equations) and data loss (matching observed data). These components combine in total loss calculation, driving backpropagation and optimization to update network parameters. The process cycles through convergence checks, where unmet criteria trigger architecture updates and parameter recalibration, while convergence enables model validation. This integrated approach ensures neural network predictions respect both observed data patterns and fundamental epidemiological principles, providing a robust framework for parameter estimation and disease forecasting with enhanced physical consistency compared to purely data-driven methods.Fig. 15Physics-Informed neural network (PINN) methodology flowchart

Physics-Informed neural network (PINN) methodology flowchart

Hyperparameter Configuration:

Neural network architecture as function approximator

Universal approximation theorem foundation

The theoretical basis stems from the universal approximation theorem:

Theorem 7.1

[111] Universal Approximation] For any continuous function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f: \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}^m$$\end{document} and ϵ > 0, there exists a neural network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{NN}_{\theta}$$\end{document} such that: 34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sup_{\mathbf{x} \in K} \| f(\mathbf{x}) - \mathcal{NN}_{\theta}(\mathbf{x}) \| < \epsilon$$\end{document}

for any compact set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K \subset \mathbb{R}^d$$\end{document}.

This guarantees that our neural network can approximate the true solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf{u}(t)$$\end{document} to arbitrary precision.

Network architecture specification

We employ a feedforward architecture with hyperbolic tangent activations: 35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm{Input:} \quad \mathbf{z}^{(0)} = t \in \mathbb{R}$$\end{document}36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{Hidden Layer 1:} \quad \mathbf{z}^{(1)} = \tanh\left(\mathbf{W}^{(1)} \mathbf{z}^{(0)} + \mathbf{b}^{(1)}\right) \in \mathbb{R}^{20}$$\end{document}37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm{Output:} \quad \mathbf{z}^{(2)} = \mathbf{W}^{(2)} \mathbf{z}^{(1)} + \mathbf{b}^{(2)} \in \mathbb{R}^{6}$$\end{document}

Architecture Rationale: Single Input: Time t enables learning temporal evolution20 Hidden Units: Balances model capacity with computational efficiencyHyperbolic Tangent: Provides smooth derivatives essential for ODE complianceLinear Output: Preserves interpretability of population proportions

Single Input: Time t enables learning temporal evolution

Single Input: Time t enables learning temporal evolution

20 Hidden Units: Balances model capacity with computational efficiency

20 Hidden Units: Balances model capacity with computational efficiency

Hyperbolic Tangent: Provides smooth derivatives essential for ODE compliance

Hyperbolic Tangent: Provides smooth derivatives essential for ODE compliance

Linear Output: Preserves interpretability of population proportions

The diagram Fig. 16 presents the neural network architecture designed for Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) modeling of Hepatitis C Virus (HCV) dynamics. The network follows a 1–20-6 configuration, accepting time (t) as the single input variable and producing six output states corresponding to HCV epidemiological compartments. The hidden layers 20 neurons respectively enable nonlinear transformation and feature extraction, capturing complex temporal patterns in disease progression. This architecture balances model complexity with computational efficiency, allowing the PINN to learn both data-driven patterns and physics-constrained relationships between input time and output states. The structured design facilitates simultaneous prediction of multiple disease variables while maintaining interpretability and adherence to biological constraints essential for epidemiological applications.Fig. 16Neural network architecture for HCV dynamics modeling

Neural network architecture for HCV dynamics modeling

Parameter initialization strategy

We employ He initialization for stable training: 38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf{W}^{(1)}_{ij} \sim \mathcal{N}\left(0, \frac{2}{d_{\mathrm{in}}}\right), \quad \mathbf{b}^{(1)}_i = 0$$\end{document}39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf{W}^{(2)}_{kl} \sim \mathcal{N}\left(0, \frac{2}{d_{\mathrm{hidden}}}\right), \quad \mathbf{b}^{(2)}_k = 0$$\end{document}

This initialization preserves activation variances through forward propagation, preventing vanishing/exploding gradients.

Residual minimization framework

Compartment-weighted objective function

To account for differences in scale, clinical importance, and data quality across compartments, we define a weighted composite loss function: 40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{total}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \sum_{m=1}^{M} \omega_m \, \mathcal{L}_m(\boldsymbol{\theta}) + \lambda \, \mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),$$\end{document}

where:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_m(\boldsymbol{\theta})$$\end{document}: Loss for compartment m, typically the mean squared error: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_m(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \frac{1}{K} \sum_{k=1}^{K} \left[ y_m(t_k) - \hat{y}_m(t_k; \boldsymbol{\theta}) \right]^2.$$\end{document}ωm: Compartment-specific weight, determined by: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega_m = \frac{1}{\sigma_m^2} \cdot \frac{I_m}{\sum_{j=1}^{M} I_j},$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma_m^2$$\end{document} is the variance of the observed data for compartment m, and Im is an importance factor (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{\mathcal{A}} > I_{\mathcal{S}}$$\end{document} to prioritize fitting the acute compartment).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\theta})$$\end{document}: Regularization term constraining parameters to biologically plausible ranges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\min} \leq \boldsymbol{\theta} \leq \boldsymbol{\theta}_{\max}$$\end{document}: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \sum_{i=1}^{P} \left[ \max(0, \theta_i - \theta_i^{\max}) + \max(0, \theta_i^{\min} - \theta_i) \right]^2.$$\end{document}λ: Regularization strength hyperparameter.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_m(\boldsymbol{\theta})$$\end{document}: Loss for compartment m, typically the mean squared error: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_m(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \frac{1}{K} \sum_{k=1}^{K} \left[ y_m(t_k) - \hat{y}_m(t_k; \boldsymbol{\theta}) \right]^2.$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_m(\boldsymbol{\theta})$$\end{document}: Loss for compartment m, typically the mean squared error: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_m(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \frac{1}{K} \sum_{k=1}^{K} \left[ y_m(t_k) - \hat{y}_m(t_k; \boldsymbol{\theta}) \right]^2.$$\end{document}

ωm: Compartment-specific weight, determined by: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega_m = \frac{1}{\sigma_m^2} \cdot \frac{I_m}{\sum_{j=1}^{M} I_j},$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma_m^2$$\end{document} is the variance of the observed data for compartment m, and Im is an importance factor (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{\mathcal{A}} > I_{\mathcal{S}}$$\end{document} to prioritize fitting the acute compartment).

ωm: Compartment-specific weight, determined by: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega_m = \frac{1}{\sigma_m^2} \cdot \frac{I_m}{\sum_{j=1}^{M} I_j},$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma_m^2$$\end{document} is the variance of the observed data for compartment m, and Im is an importance factor (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{\mathcal{A}} > I_{\mathcal{S}}$$\end{document} to prioritize fitting the acute compartment).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\theta})$$\end{document}: Regularization term constraining parameters to biologically plausible ranges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\min} \leq \boldsymbol{\theta} \leq \boldsymbol{\theta}_{\max}$$\end{document}: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \sum_{i=1}^{P} \left[ \max(0, \theta_i - \theta_i^{\max}) + \max(0, \theta_i^{\min} - \theta_i) \right]^2.$$\end{document}

Two-phase estimation strategy

Phase 1: Sensitivity-Guided Parameter Selection A global sensitivity analysis (Morris method) identifies parameters with high influence on model outputs. Parameters with low total-effect indices are fixed at literature values, while high-sensitivity parameters proceed to estimation. Phase 2: Constrained Optimization The reduced parameter set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{est}} \subset \boldsymbol{\theta}$$\end{document} is estimated by solving: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{est}}^* = \arg\min_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{est}} \in \Theta_{\mathrm{est}}} \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{total}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{est}}, \boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{fixed}}),$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta_{\mathrm{est}}$$\end{document} is the feasible space for estimated parameters, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{fixed}}$$\end{document} are literature-based fixed parameters. Optimization uses a hybrid approach:Global search via Differential Evolution to avoid local minima.Local refinement via L-BFGS-B with gradient information.

PINN-Integrated parameter estimation

When using the Physics-Informed Neural Network, the epidemiological parameters θ become trainable variables within the neural network framework. The loss function becomes: 41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}(\theta_{\mathrm{NN}}, \boldsymbol{\theta}) = \underbrace{\frac{1}{N_c} \sum_{i=1}^{N_c} \left\| \frac{d\mathbf{u}_{\theta_{\mathrm{NN}}}}{dt}(t_i) - \mathbf{f}(t_i, \mathbf{u}_{\theta_{\mathrm{NN}}}(t_i); \boldsymbol{\theta}) \right\|_2^2}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{physics}}} + \gamma \underbrace{\sum_{m=1}^{M} \omega_m \mathcal{L}_{m,\mathrm{data}}(\theta_{\mathrm{NN}})}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{data}}},$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_{m,\mathrm{data}} = \frac{1}{N_d} \sum_{j=1}^{N_d} \left[ u_{m,\theta_{\mathrm{NN}}}(t_j) - y_m(t_j) \right]^2$$\end{document}. The parameters θ and neural network weights θNN are updated simultaneously via backpropagation, enabling joint state and parameter inference in a single optimization loop.

Physics-Informed loss function: the core innovation

Our overfitting prevention strategy employs four complementary mechanisms:Physics-Informed Regularization: Intrinsic regularization through governing equationsArchitectural Constraints: Network architecture design to limit model capacityTraining Protocol Optimization: Dynamic learning strategies during trainingValidation-Based Control: Continuous monitoring and intervention

Multi-objective optimization framework

The PINN loss function represents a constrained optimization problem: 42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\min_{\theta} \mathcal{L}(\theta) = \underbrace{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{physics}}(\theta)}_{\text{ODE Compliance}} + \lambda \underbrace{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta)}_{\text{Data Fidelity}} + \mu \underbrace{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{initial}}(\theta)}_{\text{Initial Conditions}}$$\end{document}

Physics-Informed loss component

This term ensures the neural network satisfies the HCV transmission dynamics: 43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{physics}}(\theta) = \frac{1}{N_c} \sum_{i=1}^{N_c} \left\| \frac{d\mathbf{u}_{\theta}(t_i)}{dt} - \mathbf{f}(t_i, \mathbf{u}_{\theta}(t_i); \boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{epi}}) \right\|_2^2$$\end{document}

Data fidelity loss component

This term ensures agreement with available observational data: 44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{data}}(\theta) = \frac{1}{N_d} \sum_{j=1}^{N_d} \left\| \mathbf{u}_{\theta}(t_j) - \mathbf{u}_{\mathrm{data}}(t_j) \right\|_2^2$$\end{document}

Loss weighting strategy

We employ ν = 0.1 to balance physical consistency with data fitting: 45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu = \frac{\text{Importance of Physics}}{\text{Importance of Data}} = \frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{data}}^2}{\sigma_{\mathrm{physics}}^2}$$\end{document}

This heuristic weighting acknowledges that physical principles provide stronger constraints than potentially noisy data.

Comprehensive overfitting prevention strategy

Advanced training protocols for overfitting prevention

Early Stopping with Patience: We implement early stopping based on validation loss:

Learning Rate Scheduling: We employ cosine annealing with warm restarts: 46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta_t = \eta_{\min} + \frac{1}{2}(\eta_{\max} - \eta_{\min})\left(1 + \cos\left(\frac{T_{\mathrm{cur}}}{T_i}\pi\right)\right)$$\end{document}

This prevents the optimizer from getting stuck in sharp minima that may indicate overfitting.

Gradient Clipping: We implement gradient clipping to prevent exploding gradients: 47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{clipped}} = \mathbf{g} \cdot \min\left(1, \frac{\tau}{\|\mathbf{g}\|_2}\right)$$\end{document}

with clipping threshold τ = 1.0.

Data-specific overfitting controls

Synthetic Data Augmentation: For data loss component, we employ:Additive Noise: Gaussian noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{N}(0, 0.01\sigma)$$\end{document} added to training dataTemporal Jittering: Small random shifts in temporal samplingDropout in Data Loss: Randomly omit data points during training

Additive Noise: Gaussian noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{N}(0, 0.01\sigma)$$\end{document} added to training data

Temporal Jittering: Small random shifts in temporal sampling

Temporal Jittering: Small random shifts in temporal sampling

Dropout in Data Loss: Randomly omit data points during training

Dropout in Data Loss: Randomly omit data points during training

Weighted Data Loss: We use a reduced weighting for data loss to prevent over-reliance on potentially noisy data: 48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda_d = 0.1 \quad \text{(Data loss weight)}$$\end{document}

This prioritizes physical consistency over exact data fitting.

Testing on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(T_{\mathrm{val}}, T_{\mathrm{max}}]$$\end{document}

Theoretical justification

Bias-Variance Tradeoff in PINNs: The physics-informed approach inherently reduces variance by restricting hypothesis space: 49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{PINN}} \subset \mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{NN}} \subset C^1([0,T], \mathbb{R}^6)$$\end{document}

Generalization Bound: For our PINN architecture, we can bound generalization error: 50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{gen}} \leq \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{train}} + \mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\frac{N_{\mathrm{params}} \log N_c}{N_c}}\right)$$\end{document}

The regularization techniques ensure the second term remains small.

Numerical implementation and computational aspects

Derivative computation methodology

Finite Difference Scheme: We implemented a hybrid finite difference approach for derivative computation:Central Difference (interior points): 51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt}\Big|_{t_i} \approx \frac{\mathbf{u}(t_{i+1}) - \mathbf{u}(t_{i-1})}{2h}$$\end{document}Forward Difference (first point): 52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt}\Big|_{t_1} \approx \frac{\mathbf{u}(t_2) - \mathbf{u}(t_1)}{h}$$\end{document}Backward Difference (last point): 53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt}\Big|_{t_N} \approx \frac{\mathbf{u}(t_N) - \mathbf{u}(t_{N-1})}{h}$$\end{document}

Central Difference (interior points): 51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt}\Big|_{t_i} \approx \frac{\mathbf{u}(t_{i+1}) - \mathbf{u}(t_{i-1})}{2h}$$\end{document}

Central Difference (interior points): 51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt}\Big|_{t_i} \approx \frac{\mathbf{u}(t_{i+1}) - \mathbf{u}(t_{i-1})}{2h}$$\end{document}

Forward Difference (first point): 52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt}\Big|_{t_1} \approx \frac{\mathbf{u}(t_2) - \mathbf{u}(t_1)}{h}$$\end{document}

Forward Difference (first point): 52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt}\Big|_{t_1} \approx \frac{\mathbf{u}(t_2) - \mathbf{u}(t_1)}{h}$$\end{document}

Backward Difference (last point): 53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt}\Big|_{t_N} \approx \frac{\mathbf{u}(t_N) - \mathbf{u}(t_{N-1})}{h}$$\end{document}

Backward Difference (last point): 53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{dt}\Big|_{t_N} \approx \frac{\mathbf{u}(t_N) - \mathbf{u}(t_{N-1})}{h}$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h = t_{i+1} - t_i$$\end{document} is the temporal spacing between collocation points.

Automatic Differentiation Comparison: While finite differences provided sufficient accuracy for our application, we verified consistency with PyTorch’s automatic differentiation, achieving comparable results (mean absolute error < 10−4).

Optimization and training dynamics

Gradient Descent with Momentum: We employed gradient descent with Nesterov momentum for stable convergence: 54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{k+1} = \mu v_k - \eta \nabla_{\theta} \mathcal{L}(\theta_k)$$\end{document}55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta_{k+1} = \theta_k + v_{k+1}$$\end{document}

where µ = 0.9 is the momentum coefficient and η is the adaptive learning rate.

Learning Rate Schedule: We implemented cosine annealing for adaptive learning rate adjustment: 56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta_k = \eta_{\mathrm{min}} + \frac{1}{2}(\eta_{\mathrm{max}} - \eta_{\mathrm{min}})\left(1 + \cos\left(\frac{k}{K}\pi\right)\right)$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta_{\mathrm{max}} = 0.05$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta_{\mathrm{min}} = 0.001$$\end{document}, and K = 300 total epochs.

Early Stopping Criterion: Training terminated when validation loss failed to improve by at least 10−6 for 25 consecutive epochs, preventing overfitting.

Theoretical advantages and error analysis

Error decomposition framework

The total approximation error can be decomposed: 58\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{total}} = \underbrace{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{approximation}}}_{\text{NN Capacity}} + \underbrace{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{optimization}}}_{\text{Training Error}} + \underbrace{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{generalization}}}_{\text{Collocation Points}}$$\end{document}

Convergence guarantees

Under certain regularity conditions, PINNs exhibit:

Theorem 7.2

PINN Convergence For sufficiently large neural networks and adequate training, the PINN solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf{u}_{\theta}$$\end{document} converges to the true solution u: 59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim_{N_c, N_{\mathrm{params}} \to \infty} \| \mathbf{u}_{\theta} - \mathbf{u} \|_{L^2} = 0$$\end{document}

Software and data source

The numerical simulations and sensitivity analyses in this study were implemented in Python, utilizing its scientific computing libraries (NumPy, SciPy). The model was parameterized using a combination of biologically realistic values and ranges obtained from published epidemiological literature on HCV [98] which is taken from the supplemented with fitted parameters calibrated to match Zimbabwean HCV data from 1990 to 2019, ensuring the model reflects real-world transmission dynamics. The Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) was similarly developed and trained in Python, using the model’s own governing fractional differential equations as the primary data source for generating the physics-informed loss.

Training and testing metrics

The training and testing loss curves in Fig. 17 demonstrate excellent convergence behavior, with both losses decreasing steadily and stabilizing at low values (10−2 range). This indicates the Physics-Informed Neural Network effectively learned the HCV transmission dynamics without overfitting. The convergence pattern validates that the PINN successfully captured the underlying all compartment ODE system while maintaining physical plausibility. The low final loss values provide confidence in the model’s predictions for epidemiological analysis and public health decision-making.Fig. 17Training and testing loss of PINN

Training and testing loss of PINN

Distribution analyse and sensitivity analysis of reproduction number

The compartment distribution in Fig. 18a reveals the stable endemic state of HCV in the population after 100 time units. Susceptible individuals (42.2%) remain the largest group, but significantly reduced from the initial 80%, indicating substantial infection penetration. The recovered population (15.6%) has built up substantial immunity, while chronic (13.7%) and Quit treatment (8.3%) groups represent the long-term disease burden. The acute cases (4.6%) and exposed individuals (8.1%) maintain active transmission chains. This distribution confirms the persistent nature of HCV infection with all compartments maintaining non-zero populations, consistent with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{R}_0 > $$\end{document} 1 stability analysis.Fig. 18Analyse of distribution and contour plot of reproduction number. (a) Distribution analyse of all compartment. (b) Parameter sensitivity of reproduction number

Analyse of distribution and contour plot of reproduction number. (a) Distribution analyse of all compartment. (b) Parameter sensitivity of reproduction number

The Fig. 18b shows the sensitivity of the reproduction number and sensitivity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{R}_{0}$$\end{document} performs in the parameters which are β1, β2, β3, β4, γ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{r}$$\end{document}, η, δ and θ.Acute transmission rate (β2) emerges as the most powerful leverage point for control. A 1% reduction in acute transmission decreases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{R}_0$$\end{document} by 0.51%, making it the most efficient intervention target. Simultaneously, improving acute recovery rate (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{r}$$\end{document}) provides the strongest protective effect, with each 1% increase reducing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{R}_0$$\end{document} by 0.41%. The sensitivity analysis reveals that resources focused on acute phase interventions yield maximum impact, while chronic phase parameters show minimal returns. This creates a clear priority hierarchy for resource allocation in control programs.

Sensitivity analysis through Morris method

In this Morris sensitivity analysis method, we apply the sensitivity for the parameters β1, β2, β3, β4, γ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{r}$$\end{document}, η, δ and θ through PINN. The Morris sensitivity analysis in Figure 19 identifies β2 (acute transmission rate) as the most influential parameter with the highest mean elementary effect (µ ≈ 1.6), indicating it exerts the strongest direct impact on increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{R}_{0}$$\end{document}, conforming the dominant factor in HCV spread. Parameters β1 (exposed transmission) and β3 (chronic transmission) show moderate positive µ values which has moderate sensitivity, acting as transmission amplifiers. Treatment-related parameters (β4, θ, δ) demonstrate intermediately lower sensitivity, while recovery rates (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak{r}$$\end{document}, η, γ) exhibit lower but meaningful influence as transmission suppressors. This analysis confirms that acute phase transmission control should be the primary intervention focus, with treatment optimization as a secondary strategy for effective epidemic management.Fig. 19Analysis of sensitivity via Morris method

Analysis of sensitivity via Morris method

Time series error analysis

In this subsection, we analyse time series error analysis with synthetic real data and model prediction for the fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf{e}=1.0$$\end{document}.

This model’s behavior is exceptionally accurate for the Susceptible compartment, as confirmed by the goodness-of-fit plot in Fig. 20a. The near-perfect R2 score of 0.995 and very low RMSE of 0.010 show an almost exact match between the model’s prediction and the synthetic data. This means the fractional-order model reliably captures the real-world dynamics of how the susceptible population decreases over time.Fig. 20Time series analyse of susceptible compartment and Exposed compartment. The goodness-of-fit plot confirms the model in Fig. 20b is highly accurate for this compartment. The strong R2 value of 0.978 and a low RMSE of 0.010 demonstrate that the model’s predictions for the exposed population curve closely match the observed synthetic data, reliably capturing the dynamics of how individuals become infected but are not yet infectious.

The model demonstrates excellent predictive power for acute infection dynamics in Figure 21a, as confirmed by the goodness-of-fit plot. The high R2 value of 0.967 and a very low RMSE of 0.008 show a near-perfect alignment between the model’s projection and the synthetic data, validating its accuracy in simulating the rapid rise and fall characteristic of the acute infection phase.Fig. 21Time series analyse of acute compartment and chronic compartment

Time series analyse of acute compartment and chronic compartment

However, the model’s performance for this chronic compartment in Fig. 21b is notably weaker, as shown by the goodness-of-fit metrics. The R2 value of 0.867 suggests only a moderate correlation between the model’s predictions and the synthetic data, while the RMSE of 0.011 indicates a less precise fit. This implies that while the fractional-order model captures the general trend, it is less reliable for accurately predicting the specific long-term dynamics of the chronic population.

The model demonstrates strong predictive accuracy for quit treatment dynamics in Figure 22a, as confirmed by the goodness-of-fit plot. The high R2 value of 0.990 and low RMSE of 0.007 show excellent agreement between the model’s predictions and the synthetic data, validating its reliability in simulating the rapid surge and decline characteristic of quit treatment needs during an outbreak.Fig. 22Time series analyse of Quit treatment compartment and recovered compartment

Time series analyse of Quit treatment compartment and recovered compartment

The model demonstrates good predictive accuracy for recovery dynamics in Fig. 22b, as evidenced by the goodness-of-fit metrics. The strong R2 value of 0.974 and low RMSE of 0.010 indicate a reliable alignment between the model’s predictions and the synthetic data, confirming its effectiveness in capturing the cumulative nature of the recovery process over time.
